--- Primer to DevOps - Project lifecycle ---

(upbeat music)
-: So before we actually go into the usage
of Azure DevOps tools,
for the benefit of those students
who do not know why the term DevOps came in the first place,
I wanna go through some core concepts first.
So the first set of chapters are going to go into
what actually encapsulates the development
and deployment of an application.
So let's say you're working for an IT organization.
The IT organization is responsible
for maintaining all of the IT applications
and infrastructure for different business units.
Let's say one business unit
now comes to your company with a requirement
saying that they want to have an application,
that application will have certain functionality
or features that are defined by their users,
the department users.
So now it's your company's responsibility
to develop an application and deploy that application.
For developing and deploying an application,
initially you might have a project put into place.
That project would have certain requirements,
so you'd gather requirements from the users
on what features they want
to be as part of that application.
Then you have a design document,
you would have developers,
you would have a project manager
assigned onto the project itself,
and then you go ahead with the development
and deployment of the application.
Now, the project itself should follow some sort of model.
So there are different models available
by which you can base your project on.
One of the earliest models that was used
was the Waterfall Model.
Initially, this model was actually used
in manufacturing companies and construction companies,
but later on it was adopted by software engineering firms.
In this model, there are different phases.
You have phases such as the project planning phase.
After the project planning phase,
you then start gathering requirements from your users,
you then perform an analysis,
you start designing your application.
So what is the application gonna look like?
What are different screens that could come up
within the application?
If it needs to connect onto a data store,
how would you define that data store?
Then you go into the coding phase
when you actually code the application.
You then test the application,
and then finally you deploy the application.
Some of the advantages when it comes on to using
this particular type of model are...
So there is a clear demarcation
between one phase and the other.
There is one phase specifically for gathering
all of your requirements.
Once that phase is complete,
you'll go onto the analysis phase.
You will start analyzing the requirements.
You have the opportunity to solidify
all of the requirements upfront itself.
This is the most important phase.
So one of the most important phases
when you gather all of the requirements from the end users.
So what are the features that they want
as part of their application?
And it also gives the project manager
who's in charge of the entire project
clear insights on aspects such as the delivery dates,
cost estimates, et cetera.
Some of the disadvantages of this model are
the customer only gets to see the end product,
the application, at the end of the entire life cycle.
For a project, this entire model,
the collection of all of these phases,
it could take anywhere between three months to a year
or even a couple of years to complete.
If it's a complex project,
it takes time to gather the requirements.
It takes time to analyze all of the requirements.
It could take time to design the code test, et cetera.
So here, the issue is that the users, the customer,
will only see a working product, the application,
at the end of this entire project.
Now, if this takes a year,
there is so much that can actually happen in a year's time.
Let's say after four or five months,
the end users, while browsing on the internet,
they come across a similar software
that might be developed by another company, another vendor,
and that vendor is offering that users to buy the product.
Maybe the product fills only 60% of their requirements,
but still they're happy.
The users might then tell you, tell your IT department
that we just don't have time to wait
for the delivery of the software.
I mean, this could happen.
They could tell you that we'd rather now go ahead
and buy the product that's already available in the market,
and maybe the cost of the product
is less than the entire cost of this entire project.
So now this becomes an issue,
because all the time, those four to five months,
you might have reached, let's say, the design phase.
The time is lost, the cost is lost,
because now your users have found a product in the market.
Even though it does not fulfill all of the requirements,
it might be filling the key requirements
and they're more than happy to go ahead
and buy and use that software.
So one of the biggest disadvantages is
your users only get to see the working application
at the end of this entire project.
Next is changes in scope.
Yes, I did tell you that with the help of these phases,
you have a clear demarcation between the different phases.
But sometimes, let's say you are in the design phase,
your users might start adding more requirements.
You might tell them that there is a cutoff
when it comes on to requirements phase,
but then sometimes you just can't stop your users
from giving you further requirements for the application.
So now you have to go back onto probably the analysis phase,
analyze those extra requirements,
and then put it in the design phase.
And if you are in the coding phase,
it becomes even much worse,
because you might have to go back onto the analysis phase,
go back onto the design phase,
and then go back on to the coding phase.
The reason for this is, again,
the amount of time it takes
to go through each of the phases.
I said over time, the users might think of more requirements
that need to be embedded in the application.
Another disadvantage is the bugs that could occur
in the testing phase.
So let's say that you are testing the application.
You have a set of testers who are testing the application
and they might also be tested by a small set of end users.
Let's say the end users during this phase tell you
that one of the screens is not as it should be
when it comes onto the application,
and they tell you this is how the screen should look like.
Because of this,
you might need to go back onto the design phase.
Could be.
Maybe you could go just to the coding phase
and make a small change onto the code.
But if it is a drastic change,
you have to go back again onto design,
change the code, and then again, test.
Again, it takes time,
and all of this could delay the project even further.
So in this journey, in wanting to explain to you
the reason behind DevOps itself,
I wanna start right from the beginning
in how earlier projects actually adopted the Waterfall Model
when it came onto the development
and deployment of an application.

--- Primer to DevOps - Agile Methodology ---

(bright music)
-: So in the last chapter, I explained to you
some of the advantages and disadvantages
when it came onto the Waterfall model.
So told you earlier projects in software engineering firms
normally adopted the Waterfall model,
and probably some variations of this model
when it came onto a project encapsulating
the development and deployment of an application.
Then we came to the birth of the Agile software development.
So in the year 2001, 17 software developers
met to discuss a lighter development method
and came up with a manifesto,
when it came onto Agile software development.
So because of the Waterfall model,
because of the inherent problems with the Waterfall model,
they want to come up with a different model
when it came onto software development.
I've taken this entire term
when it comes onto the Agile practice from Wikipedia.
This is the definition in Wikipedia,
when it comes onto the Agile practice
for software development.
It includes a lot of terms here.
Let me explain this one by one.
Before that in a nutshell, what Agile does is
it's kind of an iterative process,
wherein you go through the requirements.
You then do the design, development, testing,
deployment and review, and then you repeat the cycle again.
So in an Agile practice, you take your entire project,
and you kind of break it up into pieces.
So instead of phases, you break it up into pieces,
and then you tackle each piece one by one.
And each piece is the entire cycle
when it comes on to gathering the requirements,
doing the design, doing the development,
doing the testing, deployment, and review.
Also, when it comes on to building your teams,
in the normal Waterfall model,
when you have a project, so you'll have the project manager,
you'll have the people assign onto the project.
There, those people are only dedicated
for the project itself.
But when it comes onto the Agile practice,
here you have the use of self-organizing
and cross-functional teams,
wherein you have people from different functional areas
working together as a team
when it comes on to building an application.
Also, there is a close coordination of collaboration
between the teams, the customers, and the end users.
In the Waterfall model, if we go back on top,
sometimes what happens is the project team
will only interact with the users
during the requirements phase.
That's it.
And then there could be an entire disconnect
until the testing phase, wherein they give a model
of the application onto the user for initial testing.
So there's kind of a gap.
Yes, companies can have variations
of the model wherein during the end of each phase,
they could meet up with the end user.
But again, this depends upon where the company
is actually following that variation of that model.
So sometimes there is a gap.
The users are only met during the requirements phase
and then the testing phase.
And I said there's a lot that could happen during this time.
But in the Agile practice, it is a constant collaboration
between the cross functional teams,
the customers, and the end users.
The entire purpose also of Agile
is early delivery of the software.
So not software at the end of the entire project,
but software in increments.
So you have an initial deployment of the software
and then you build on the software in increments
and then give it on to the end users.
Here, you have continual improvements onto the software,
and if there are any sort of change in requirements
or understanding, you can be more flexible
on how you respond to the end user.
How do you actually incorporate the functionality
within the software itself?
So instead of being stringent, instead of following
a stringent process in the Waterfall model,
here, you are trying to be more dynamic in nature,
more flexible in nature
when it comes onto the Agile methodology.
So in this chapter, want to introduce the Agile methodology.
This is important when it comes on to DevOps.
In the next chapter, I want to talk about Scrum.
This is one of the Agile development
methodologies that is available.


=== Design and Implement Infrastructure as Code ===


--- Primer to DevOps - Scrum process ---

(upbeat music)
-: So in the last chapter, I talked about the agile practice.
So this is kind of a practice.
Now based on this practice, there was,
now based on this practice,
there is an agile development methodology known as scrum.
There are different agile methodologies in place.
It all depends on what suits your purpose.
Here, it is giving an overall diagram
when it comes onto the entire scrum process.
So it's the same case wherein, again,
you have your business department
coming to your company
saying that they want to build an application
for the end users
and the application needs to have certain functionality,
it needs to have certain features.
Now, instead of actually collecting
all of the requirements at one time,
here, what you do is you collect the requirements
in terms of user stories.
User stories is just the features which the users want,
but this is written in their terms.
You know, you basically look at
how do you want to have the features in place
from the user standpoint.
You define them as something known as user stories
when it comes onto the scrum process.
The vision is what you want to have
in the end as a working product.
You then take these user stories,
based on these stories, you now add items.
These are kind of your to do list
for the application development.
So what needs to be done
in order to fulfill these user stories.
These are added onto something known as a product backlog.
Then you start taking a selected set of these items
that need to be done from the product backlog
and you create something known as a sprint.
That sprint has its own backlog.
Let's say I said in the beginning,
you've met with your users
and they have given you five user stories.
So these are probably five features, initial features,
important features that need to be part of an application.
Based on this user stories,
the team has decided that as part of the product backlog,
10 items need to be implemented
in order to fulfill these five user stories.
Out of these 10 items, let's say three important items.
What is really, really required
is taken as part of a sprint.
That is added onto something known as a sprint backlog.
So the sprint is the entire process about
taking those items,
developing the application with those items,
testing the items,
and deploying those items.
You know, deploying the application with those items.
Normally, a sprint is taken as just a two week process,
maximum of four weeks.
So here, the key difference between this process
and the waterfall model is instead of spending a month
gathering the requirements,
instead of spending a month now after that
analyzing the requirements,
instead of spending now another month
creating a design based on the analysis,
here on a continual basis,
you are taking user stories from users.
You are then creating items based on those stories
into something known as a product backlog.
You then have a team that picks out items,
the important items, from the product backlog
and puts them into something known as a sprint backlog.
So these, again, items that need to be implemented.
Then a kind of a small mini project is underway
wherein they develop the application with those items.
They then test the application.
All of this is done within that two week period
and then it is deployed.
And once a sprint is complete,
the entire process is started again.
You'll pick out the next items from the product backlog.
You put it in the next sprint,
so you'll have another sprint now,
again, a two week duration,
and the items within those sprints
are implemented accordingly.
And then in each sprint, you'll have basically a review
wherein you showcase the items that have been done
in the sprint,
and then you have a retrospective
wherein the teams come together to see what worked best
and what did not work,
so that you can actually continuously improve
how things are managed within the sprint itself.
So here are the entire purpose, again,
when it comes on to the scrum process,
which is nothing but an agile development methodology,
is to break up the project into multiple pieces.
So instead of actually taking everything as a whole,
everything in one shot, you take the requirements in phases.
You then implement those requirements,
you go back, you take probably more requirements,
you create items out of the requirements,
and then you implement those items.
This is not only good when it comes onto
the initial development of an application,
but for future releases as well.
See, remember the first thing is obviously,
developing an application and giving it to the end users.
But then after that, over time,
you have to maintain the software.
You have to maintain the application.
Because see, what you also have to remember is
when you complete a project using the waterfall model,
right, you've deployed the application,
your users are happy.
Now after that,
the users might still ask to add more features.
That's normal.
So again, for that,
you might have a small mini project wherein, again,
you gather the requirements for those new features,
you perform analysis, you perform design,
you are recording, you are testing, and you're deployment.
Again, it won't be a full blown project like a year.
It could just be probably three to four weeks
wherein you actually add those new features.
Well, the addition of new features works very well
also in the scrum process.
In this case, again,
you would take those new features as user stories,
add them to the product backlog,
then add them to a sprint backlog,
and then implement it accordingly.
So all you have to understand is these scrum process,
or the agile methodology in whole,
is used as an IT process
when it comes to delivering an application.

in a quicker fashion.
Now, let's come onto the next hurdle.
Let's say a company is using the Waterfall model
for a project.
So let's assume the development team
has developed the application, testing is complete,
and now the application is deployed onto production.
Users are happy.
You then have an operations team in every company.
They take care of day to day operations.
You might have a server operation team
that manages all the servers.
You could have a database operation team
that takes care of all of the databases.
Now, let's say that your users, your business department
has asked for new features to be added onto the application.
Now, normally during an initial project
when you have a project in hand,
normally the project would have the budget

But then after that maybe
there might not be enough budget in place

Again, this can get quite complex
just taking a use case scenario.

to deliver the new feature.
Now, this can put a strain on the developers
who are responsible for adding new features.
See, the problem is when you add new features
onto an application, you don't know
if it might break existing features in the application.
That's why you need to be extra careful
when adding new features.
Testing is very important.
So sometimes developers might try a quick way
in implementing the feature
because they might have a time constraint.
And because of produce budget,
sometimes testing times may not be enough
for testing the application.
These are all real world problems that can occur

Now, during this time, I've seen that
operations teams are well aware of these issues

That's why they might be hesitant to deploy the changes
of the application onto production based environments.
Because see, in the end the operations team
are the one in the line of fire.
If something happens to the application,

of the application, for the uptime of the service,
for the uptime of the databases.
The users will start complaining.
That is why they're always hesitant
and there is always this constant tussle
between the operations team and the development team.
The development team will say they have added the feature,
they have tested it, just go ahead and deploy it.
But the operations team will say,
have you done enough testing?
Have you gone to, let's say the security department
to ensure that whatever feature you have added
is secure when it comes onto the entire application?
Are you sure you have not added any sort of vulnerability
to the application because of the feature you have added?
And this is always because of the disconnect.
See, the development team will be working in isolation,
adding the feature to the application.
And your operations team is always sitting separately
only looking at operations.
Neither team knows what the other team is doing
and that's where we come to the term of DevOps itself.
So let's mark first and end onto this chapter
wherein we have looked at the hurdles
between the development and the operations team.

--- What is source control ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi and welcome back.
So, now before we go into the working of the Git software.
So, I told you, first we look at the working of Git
then we look at the working of GitHub,
and then as your repos.
For the benefit of those students
who are not familiar with the version control system,
I wanna give you what is a core benefit
of having a tool when it comes onto source control.
A version control system as the name does imply,
is used for maintaining different versions
of your application.
A version system as the name does imply,
helps to maintain different version for your files.
For example, you might have three files on your system.
When you create the file it'll be in its original version,
it could be version one.
Then let's say you make a change onto file B.
Then it'll actually create a newer version of that file
because you have changed the contents of the file
and so on and so forth.
In case if you didn't have
a version control system in place,
and you want to maintain versions of your files,
you might then need to create folders
to actually indicate the different versions
of the files itself.
And this is a painstaking process
and it's not easy to maintain
once your files starts to grow,
once the number of people who start working
on the files also starts to grow.
Hence, you need some sort of tool
to maintain different versions of your files.
And it also helps to revert back onto a prior version.
And then using a version control tool is very important
when it comes onto maintaining application code.
Let's say you have a set of developers
who are working on application code,
and they have made a number of changes onto the code itself.
Let's say one of the developers has actually made a mistake
and they want to go back onto a previous change.
How can they do that?
They can do that with the help of a version control tool.
With the help of that version control tool
they can actually go back onto a previous change,
get back the code at that point in time.
So in this chapter
I just first initially want to give the core benefit
of having a version control tool in place.
We are gonna look at subsequent chapters
on how do we work with Git as a software
which is the most popular tool when it comes
onto source code version control.

--- What is Git ---

(upbeat music)
-: So in the last chapter
I just gave a brief introduction
when it came on to our version control system.
Now in this chapter, I just wanna go through
why we are gonna be using Git as a source control tool.
So Git is the most popular used version control system.
So a lot of companies actually employ the use of Git
in one form or the other
when it comes on to source code version control.
And this is primary because of its distribute nature.
So Git has become famous because it has a lot of features.
One of them is the distribute nature
when it comes on to the usage of Git.
Now, there are other version control systems
that behave in a different fashion.
There is a version control system
that's known as Subversion.
Now when using this particular version control system,
the developers would have a client
installed on their system.
There will be a central server
that will be hosting the Subversion server software.
And it would also host, let's say the application code.
Now, whenever a developer needs to make a change on to,
let's say a module within the application code
or certain files, they would perform the operation
of first checkout the files that are required.
The checkout would be done on their local system.
Then changes would be made on to the code.
And once that is done, they could check in the code again
with the required changes on to the server.
Then when another developer
needs to go and make a modification on to the code,
again, they would check out the required files
from the central server,
make the change and then check in the code again.
One of the drawbacks when it comes on to using
this sort of centralized system
is if the central server were to go down,
then developers would not have the ability
to code and check out the files that are required.
And that's why we have Git,
which is a distributed system,
wherein each developer,
they would also have the Git client tool installed
but they could go ahead and have their own copy
of the application code and make changes as required,
and Git would maintain the versioning
when it came on to the application code.
And apart from that, Git also has better overall performance
and it can easily manage aspects
such as creating branches, et cetera.
Now in the next chapter,
we're gonna go ahead and see how we can install Git.

of your files or basically your entire system.
Another core aspect is the working of branches
within a version control system.
And with get it manages branches in a very efficient manner.
It's very easy to go ahead and create a branch
and start working out of the branch.
Now, before we actually go into working with branches,
let me explain to you why do we have this concept
of branches in the first place.
Let's say that you have an entire team
that's working on a code for an application.
There is only one branch, your main branch.
So when you are actually making changes
onto a Git repository, by default it creates
something known as a main branch.
Because when we install the Git tool
we told that when you create a branch,
give a name of main onto the branch.
You will do your initial commit,
maybe of your application code and then your developers
will start making changes onto the
main branch itself for your code.
Now, for your application code,
you might have developers that could be working
on bug fixes for the application code
and probably adding new features for your application code.
Maybe the business users send requests
onto the I.T department to basically
add new features for the application
and then your developers start writing new code.
Now, it's always good to segregate
the changes made while
adding new features onto your code
or maybe working with bug fixes for your code.
And this segregation can be done with the help of branches.
What you can do is within Git,
you have your main branch in place
when a new feature needs to be developed
for your application.
What you do is you create a new branch
out of your main code.
So whatever is your code at that point in time
it's kind of a copy is being made
and then you can start adding or changing code to
add that new feature has a new separate branch.
See, where is this useful?
So let's say till this point in time
you have your working code
and this code, application code is
currently running in production.
Normally, you don't want to make
changes directly onto the score
because let's say you add the wrong change.
Yes, you can go back onto a prior version
to get the working copy, but has a good practice now.
You should create kind of a new
working copy of your code has a branch
then start adding the new feature by adding code.
And once you test out the feature
it's working has it should be.
You can then merge the code that is this branch
with your main branch and your branch will be
up to date with this new feature.
And then along the line you can go ahead and again
create a new branch for the next feature.
Working with branches is very, very important
and in Git you cam very easily go ahead
and create a new branch and start working out of that
new branch.

--- More on merges ---

(upbeat music)
Instructor: Hi and welcome back.
Now, in the prior chapters,

the merging process.
One way was from Visual Studio
wherein as another developer,
we had first cloned the repository,
we had made a change, and then we had done a merge.
At that point in time, when we were doing the merge
there was a conflict and we had to make sure

before we do the entire merging process.
So that is what we had done.
All of this was being done on the main branch.
This was from within Visual Studio.
Another way in which we saw the merging process
was from within Visual Studio Code.
This was very earlier on
wherein we were dealing with very simple text based files.
At that point in time,
we had created actually a feature branch
and then we merged that feature branch onto the main branch.
This was another way of performing the merging process.
But at this point in time, we were merging branches.
In the earlier example,
we were just merging the code within the same branch itself.
I will take the example wherein we had done
the merging process from within Visual Studio Code.
This sort of merging, which we had seen earlier on is known
as a fast-forward merge.
What is an example of a fast-forward merge?
So we had our main branch
and then we created a feature branch.
Within the feature branch we added the file C.txt
and then we merged the feature branch with the main branch.
Here, when we made changes onto the feature branch,
at that point in time, we did not make any changes
onto the main branch.
So the feature branch, the changes were made
but at the same time, no one did any changes
onto the main branch during this point in time.
So when Git wanted to do a merging process,
it did something known has a fast-forward merge
wherein the main branch just now pointed
onto to the last commit of the feature branch.
It is like taking the pointer and then using
the commit of the feature branch to perform
that fast-forward merge.
I'll show you an example of another merge
so that you actually understand this concept
when it comes onto a fast-forward merge.
Here, just remember that when we were doing
the merging process between two branches,
we made changes in one branch that is the feature branch
but we did not make any changes onto the main branch.
When we did a merge, Git saw that there is only changes
made onto one branch and not onto the other.
So it did the most fastest way of doing a merge
by just placing the pointer
onto the branch wherein the change was made.
And by pointer, I mean the pointing of the head
when it comes onto the main and the feature branch
after the merch process was complete.
Here you can see the head is pointing
onto the commit operation wherein we added file C
onto the feature branch.
If you wanna see this kind of
tree diagram for your commit operations,
from within Visual Studio Code you can do a git log
and you can use Graph
and you will see the changes accordingly.
So in this chapter,
I just wanted to talk a little bit more
when it comes on to fast-forward merging.
In the next chapter, let's look at another merging process.

that's happening onto the main branch itself.

the git history for your feature branches.
You have an option when it comes onto the merging process

please do a squash merge so that we don't have

--- Note on data recovery ---

(upbeat intro music)
-: Now in this chapter, I just wanna give a quick note.
When it comes onto maintenance and data recovery in Git.
In this documentation page,
it turns that Git automatically runs it's auto GC command.
It's like a garbage collection command.
See, when you start making a lot of commits
onto your repository, there may be some commits
that were linked onto objects,
that no longer correspond onto the history
of that Git repository.
So there may be some objects that now
are not being used at all,
or are not being tracked as part of the repository,
but they are still there.
This can happen over time when you're doing several commits
for the repository itself.
Hence, Git will occasionally see
if there are any objects that are not being referenced
and then go ahead and delete them accordingly
because you don't want to have your repository
with objects that are not being version controlled.
You can also run this command manually as well.
On this page,
you have a lot of information
when it comes on to data recovery.
Another good command when it comes on
to kind of compressing your repository,
ensuring that it does not have any sort of stale objects,
is the Git Prune command,
the other the command that can be used
to remove stale objects.
So in this chapter,
just kind of want to give these important notes
when it comes on to maintenance and data recovery in Git.

--- Note on large files in Git ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi and welcome back.
Now, in this chapter,
I just want to talk about large files
when it comes onto your Git repository.
Up 'til now, we have seen simple text based-files
and we have also looked at code within Visual Studio.
Here, I'm just giving examples
when it comes onto the documentation given for GitHub
when it comes onto hosting large files.
See, normally in your code repositories
you should not be hosting large binary-based files.
And what do I mean by this?
See, in Visual Studio, you have your code files in place.
When you build your project
into an application that can run,
that build which gets created by Visual Studio
should ideally not be part of version control.
It's your code that needs to be part of version control
because that is what you change.
But the application that gets built out of it,
that binary files, those set of binary files
that will probably host on another server to run,
has an application,
that should ideally not be a part of version control.
That's why in Visual Studio
you have this gitignore file by default
that ensures that these sort of files
are not put as part of version control.
But apart from that, if you do have a requirement
for having large files as part of your repository,
maybe you might have videos, images, et cetera.
There are options wherein you can use a tool
known as Git LFS.
This is actually used,
you need to install the tool first.
It is efficient in managing large files
as part of your repository.
What you can do here with the help of this tool,
so we're gonna go into depth into usage of this tool.
But I just wanna give an overview.
What you can do is with the help of this tool,
you can host your large objects in another place
and this tool can actually reference those large objects

--- Branching strategies ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi, and welcome back.
Now, in this chapter I just wanna give quick notes again
when it comes onto branching strategies.
So we've already talked about this before
wherein you have your main branch
which will normally contain your production code,
your working production code,
and always ensure that you have high quality
for your main branch.
Make sure there are no sort of issues or defects
in the code that's present in the main branch
because at any point in time you might want to deploy
the code
from the main branch onto a particular environment.
It should contain a working copy of your production code.
And then when you want to have any sort of features
or bug fixes for your application code,
then create separate feature branches
and then you can merge the feature branches
onto your main branch,
and make sure that you use pull request.
We've already seen the use of pull request
in both GitHub and in Azure Repos.
We have seen how we can have reviews also in place,
you know, in terms of branch policies.
This is available, again, both in GitHub
and in Azure Repos.
When you're creating feature branches,
make sure that they are not too long, basically, you know?
Keep them short-lived just for that feature itself.
Make the changes,
merge onto the main branch
and then you can go ahead and probably delete
the feature branch if it's no longer required.
Another thing that you can do when it comes onto,
let's say, merging different releases of your application...
So on one side you could have your main branch
which has your working code for your application.
When you have a particular release of your application,
let's say version one or version two,
we've seen that we can have tagging in place
when it comes onto versioning of your particular commits,
but you could also have a separate branch
that is only dedicated for a particular release.
You can make a copy of the release branch
and then of the release branch,
you can create another feature branch.
See, sometimes companies might need to have
their application delivered to multiple customers
and each customer might want to have
different feature requirements for the application code.
So let's say you have one application
in place as a company,
but then you are delivering that same application
to multiple customers
and each customer might be having their own requirements
when it comes to adding new features.
Hence, you might have the main branch has,
you know, a working copy of your code
and you might have separate release branches
for each customer,
and for each release branch
you might have different feature branches.
It all depends on the complexity
of your requirements, you know?
How do you actually manage your application code base?
But always in this particular scenario,
you have complexity in place when you have release branches,
feature branches and your main branch
because you have to ensure that your feature branch
at some point in time is obviously merged
onto your release branch,
and then your release branch might need to be merged
onto your main branch.
So you have to maintain multiple branches
in this particular aspect,
but this is something that you can also do
when it comes onto a branching strategy.
So in this chapter,
kind of again wanna go through some important aspects
when it comes onto branching.

--- Team Foundation Version Control ---

(upbeat music)
-: Now, in this chapter,
we are gonna go through in brief
about Team Foundation Version Control.
We have been focusing primary on Git because
that is the most popular source code versioning tool.
But I just wanna go in brief
on Team Foundation Version Control.
This is a separate version control tool
that was developed by Microsoft some time ago.
This is a centralized version control tool.
This is in contrast to Git
which is a distribute version control tool.
With Team Foundation Version Control,
a team member only has one version
of each file on their local machine.
The historical data for the files is held on the server.
Again, different when it comes onto to working with Git.
When you clone a project from a Git repository,
it not only has the files in place
but it also has all of the historical data.
So, if any comments were made onto the repository
that would also be stored.
But in the case of Team Foundation Version Control,
all of the historical data is stored on the server.
Now, before developers
can actually make changes onto a file,
they first need to check out the file from the server.
When it comes onto each file,
you can then also apply permissions at the file level.
So, this is different.
When you look at Git,
you can apply permissions either
at the repository level
or at the branch level.
Even in a Azure repost,
we could see security permissions
that could be applied either at the repository level
or at the branch level.
But when it comes on Team Foundation Version Control,
you can apply permissions at the file level also.

--- What is Continuous Integration ---

(upbeat music)
-: So in this particular section,
I told you that we are going to be covering build pipelines.
Now, build pipelines is part of a process
that's known as continuous integration.
The term of continuous integration implies onto being able
to automatically build and test code every time
a team member commits code changes onto version control.
In the prior section, we had seen
how we could store our code in abortion control system.
Basically Git,
we saw how we could use a remote repository
like GitHub and Azure Repos,
and every time we made a code change,
we could commit that code change onto our main branch
or maybe another feature branch onto our Git repository.
Now, the next step is obviously a developer would want
to build that application and run that application.
If a team is working on developing a web application,
they would want to have a build of that web application
in place and that build be deployed on, let's say, a server
so that users across the world could access
that web application.
Now, for the benefit of those students who are coming in
from an easy 104 background, write as your administration.
I briefly want to go
through this build and deployment process
before we go back onto continuous integration.
Why?
Because understanding the build process is very important
when it comes on to building your build pipelines,
when it comes onto continuous integration.
In the last chapter,
we have been publishing our code
onto a Git repository from Visual Studio.
Now, within Visual Studio itself, on your local machine,
you also have the ability to build your application.
When you actually run your application in Visual Studio,
what happens, it also builds your application as well.
The build tool actually builds the project
and its dependencies into a set of binaries.
These binaries can then run on the target machine.
Now, why is all of this required?
See, when you're developing a program,
this could be in Java, this could be in C#.
The language which you're developing in, it's easy for you
to understand how to basically code in
that particular language and you understand
how to implement a particular functionality,
but when you want to run that application on a machine,
remember the machine cannot understand
a programming language.
In the end,
the machine only understands how to run binary instructions.
Hence, there should be some way
to correlate your code, your application code,
into instructions that can be understood
by the underlying machine.
That's where the first step is
to build your application into a form
that can then be taken by something known as, let's say,
you're developing a .NET application.
You will have the .NET runtime running on the machine,
that would take the build of your code
and then interpret it into binary instructions
that can be understood by the underlying machine.
Hence, the first step is to take your code
and convert it on to a build
that could then be run on a target machine.
That target machine needs to have .NET also installed,
then you would only need to have the .NET runtime in place.
Normally, for a language, you will have the SDK,
which is a software development kit.
You create your code using this software development kit,
and when you want to run your code, you have another runtime
that needs to be installed on the machine.
So the software development kit actually allows you
to develop the application
and the runtime helps you to run your application.
So I said, in the next couple of chapters,
I just want to quickly go
through the build and the release process
because this is very important when you want
to understand the concept of continuous integration,
and then in the next section,
the concept of continuous deployment.

--- Now going back to continuous integration ---

(upbeat music)
Instructor: So now I wanna quickly come back
onto the concept of continuous integration.
So continuous integration is the automatic building
and testing of your code every time a team member commits
code changes onto version control.
See, in our case, when we are working
with Visual Studio as just a sole person,
it's very easy to go ahead and build your application
and probably publish your application.
But you have to remember that within an organization,
you'll have multiple teams working on an application.
If you have a large application, you might have a subset
of developers who are working on the main application code.
You could have a subset of developers that are working
on bug fixes for let's say, application code
that could be in the production environment.
You could have a subset of developers that are working
on new features that need to be added onto an application.
You have to remember that you could have the scenario
in place wherein you have different developers working
on different aspects for your application.
Now let's take a use case scenario.
Let's say one of the development teams
has 8 changes that need to be in place for an application.
And having these changes in place
could take around three weeks.
Once all the changes are done,
the developer now wants to commit these code changes
onto the git repository, the remote git repository.
Let's say they are having a feature branch in place.
They then have a pull request,
they combine the feature branch code changes
to the main branch.
And now the application needs to be built and then deployed.
Now during the build process, let's say an issue comes up.
So after the build you might do some testing.
Now because there were so many changes that took place
in this three weeks of time
and there probably could have been changes made
by another team during this time duration itself.
It could be difficult to go ahead
and diagnose where exactly the issue lies.
Is the issue that the application is not working based
on these 8 changes that were taken by this team?
Or is the issue coming up
because the changes made by this team is not compatible
with changes made by another team?
And this is because we are taking a bulk
of the changes to the application.
And then after a long duration of time
we are combining those changes
with our main application code and then testing our code.
The ideal scenario is to go ahead and take each major change
and then commit it onto the code repository,
build the application, and make sure that it works.
So if there is one change that just takes
around maybe two days, after the second day
go ahead and merge that change
with the main branch in your code repository.
Build your application and make sure that it works.
Instead of taking everything as a bulk
and then trying to see if it works,
go ahead and make these changes on a continued basis,
hence continuous integration.
Now automation is very key over here.
We should not be manually trying to go ahead
and build the application, run the application
for each and every change.
This would not be the right approach.
That's where we need to have automation in place.
So as soon as a developer publishes the code changes
onto let's say, the main branch in that git repository,
the code needs to be built automatically
and the code needs to be tested automatically as well.
If everything is done in an automated fashion
it becomes easier to ensure that more
and more changes get published onto the code repository.
They get built faster and they get tested faster.
And this is the entire concept
behind continuous integration.

--- What are Azure Pipelines ---

(upbeat music playing)
-: So, now we are gonna see how do we use Azure Pipelines
within Azure DevOps.
With Azure Pipelines, you get automation in place
when you can actually go and build
and test your code projects.
You can use Azure Pipelines for both continuous integration
and continuous delivery.
It has a support for variety of programming languages
which includes Python, Java, PHP, Ruby, and C#.
You can also deploy your application onto multiple targets
or environments with the help of Azure Pipelines.
In this particular section, we are gonna be looking
at build pipelines when we build our application code.
And then in the subsequent section,
we look at the deployment aspect.
When defining your build pipelines in Azure Pipelines,
there are two ways
that you can actually go about doing this.
First is to define your pipeline as a YAML file.
So, YAML is just a human-friendly
data serialization language
that is available for all programming languages.
It's just a convenient way
in which you can actually define your data,
you can structure your data so that it can be understood
in a human readable format.
Hence, we can define our pipeline using the YAML syntax.
Here, the file will initially
be called Azure Pipeline.yaml,
and will be created and version controlled
along with your code.
When you create your pipeline using the YAML syntax,
it'll also be added onto your Git repository
within a Azure Repos.
The benefit of this is it allows you to identify now changes
within your pipeline that could break the deployment.
So you can go back onto previous versions
of your pipeline to see what is the working version
of your pipeline if a later on change
is actually breaking the entire pipeline process.
So, one way is to define your pipeline
using the YAML syntax, and we'll be focusing a lot on this.
So there's a lot of stress
when it comes on to using as Azure Pipelines
using the YAML syntax when it comes onto the exam.
We can also use the classic interface
when it comes on to define the pipeline.
This gives you a graphical interface.
Here, you can define your pipelines
with the use of this interface
and you can run your pipeline accordingly.
Now, in the next chapter,
let's see how we can start working with Azure Pipelines.

--- Self-hosted agent ---

(bright upbeat music)
-: Hi, and welcome back.
Now, in the prior chapters, we had gone ahead

when it comes on to some sort of compute agent
or compute machine for building a pipeline.
Here, the entire pipeline
is making use of the Microsoft hosted agent
and this is probably the simplest way to run a pipeline.
A fresh virtual machine is great
for each new job of the pipeline, and after the job is run,
the virtual machine is discarded.
Here, the biggest advantage is you get an agent
that has the capability of running your build pipeline,
and then all of the maintenance and upgrades
are handled by Microsoft.
So, all of the important security upgrades
when it comes onto the agent itself.
So the template that is used to build your agent
only has the right security patches in place,
so you don't need to worry
about maintaining that agent, right?
But then obviously, you'll be having the use case
wherein you might want to build your own agent.
There are various reasons for this.
Firstly, you might want to persist the builds.
I told you in the Microsoft self hosted agent,
the machine is destroyed at the end of the pipeline.
That means it budges,
it basically deletes all of your builds.
But let's say you want to have the builds
stored on a machine.
For this, you can create your own self hosted agent,
or maybe, you have some custom software
that needs to be installed that's required for the build.
You might be having a lot of third party components
as part of your application, and these need to be in place.
In this case also, you can use a self hosted agent
or maybe after the build process,
the build needs to be probably sent onto another location.
This could be on another server
in your on-premises environment.
At that time also,
you can look towards creating a self hosted agent.
Now, in the next set of chapters,
we are going to look at how to work with self hosted agents.

--- Security at every stage ---

(boppy intro music)
Instructor: Hi and welcome back.
Now, in the (inaudible) chapters
we had seen how we could work with our build pipelines.
Now, in the subsequent chapters
we're gonna see how we can incorporate testing
and security within our build pipeline.
When it comes on testing and when it comes onto security,
it's very important that you embed these concept
at every stage in your life cycle.
See, when you're building, for example,
a web application and your abusers on the internet
who are gonna access the web application.
You know that there are so many internet-based threats
that can affect the application itself
and you have to be prepared.
The organization needs to have plans in place
to mitigate such threats.
Now, you should not think about testing your application
or even looking at the security of the application
after you deploy the application.
No. It should never be an afterthought.
You have to start right from the planning stage.
When you start collecting, for example,
your user stories - at that point in time
you have to understand how can you incorporate security?
How can you incorporate testing within your stages
that can align with the user stories
that will correspond onto application functionality.
Then during the development stage, you know,
developers, for example, if they're using Visual Studio
for building a Dark Net application,
there are plugins in the market -
security plugins that actually integrate
with the development environment.
You have plugins that integrate
with Visual Studio, with Eclipse
and what these tools can do is:
they can actually look for potential issues
within the code itself.
This is very helpful
during the development life cycle itself.
Then you can also conduct peer reviews.
For example, a senior developer
or maybe even another engineer
can look at the code that has been developed
and give any sort of advice on how the code can be improved.
And obviously,
if the organization has any sort of coding standards,
if you adhere to those coding standards
then automatically you are building quality code.
And then during the build stage
your continuous integration stage
you can also have tests embedded within your pipeline.
For example, you can have unit test in place.
You can also make use of static code analysis
within your pipeline itself
that can look at possible issues in the code.
And then when you deploy onto, let's say,
a test environment or a staging environment
you can conduct testing from a system level.
For example, you could have low testing in place.
You can conduct penetration testing
you can conduct infrastructure testing.
So testing and security are very important
at every stage of the life cycle.
In these subsequent chapters,
we're gonna look at some of the tools
that can actually help in these aspects,
especially when it comes onto continuous integration.

licensing issues.

--- Parallel jobs ---

(bright upbeat music)
-: Hi, and welcome back.
Now in this chapter, I just wanna give a quick note
when it comes onto parallel jobs.
Now, when we've been running our pipelines,
we have only had one job in place,
but as part of your pipelines,
you can actually define multiple jobs
within a pipeline itself.
And in a large organization,
you might have multiple pipelines
that could be running at the same time.
Now, when you create a free account as we have done,
initially we could not even run one pipeline,
we had to send a request onto Microsoft
so that they could allow that grant
of running a particular job.
But even at that point in time,
they only allow us to run one job at a time.
In a large organization, this could be an issue.
If they have a lot of pipelines
that need to run at the same time
or if they are multiple jobs
that may need to run in parallel,
they may not be able to do this
because they are only allowed one job at a time.
So then, the organization should look
towards purchasing additional parallel jobs.
If I go onto my organization
and go onto organization settings.
If I go onto pipelines and parallel jobs,
here you can see that is part of Microsoft hosted.
Since we have private projects, we are on the free tier,
we are only allowed one parallel job,
and that too we have a limit of how many minutes
are allowed per month.
Here you can see how much we have consumed.
Again, when it comes onto the self hosted jobs,
again, we only have one parallel job in place.
If we need more parallel jobs,
then we have to go ahead and purchase them accordingly.
In the chapter, I just want to give this important note
that if you want to run multiple jobs at a time,
you need to go ahead and purchase licenses
for running these parallel jobs.

--- Note on pipeline caching ---

(upbeat jazzy music)
Instructor: Hi, and welcome back.
Now, in this chapter,
I just wanna give a quick note
when it comes on to pipeline caching.
Now, this is used to actually reduce the build time.
See, what happens is when you have an application
that has multiple packages, see for example in .NET,
I told you that you might have NuGet packages
in place for your project
and a solution might be having multiple NuGet packages.
In order to probably restore this entire solution
this entire project and get the NuGet packages,
the packages actually have to be downloaded
from the internet.
Now, because these packages are on the internet
and they need to be downloaded on the agent,
this could increase the overall build time
for your pipeline.
Then what you should consider
is the use of pipeline caching.
What this does, is it can actually cache those packages
so that they can be reused in subsequent runs.
In each run, downloading the package over and over again
since you know that those are the packages
that are going to be reused
in multiple pipeline runs,
go ahead and download those packages
and simply add a cache task.
This is how you can actually go about doing this.
In this chapter,
I just wanna give this important note
on the concept of having pipeline caching in place.

--- Understanding deployment ---

(funky dance music)
-: Hi and welcome back.
Now, in the prior section we had seen
how we could create bills of our application,
really help our Azure Pipelines.
Well, now it's time to go ahead
and look at the deployment of our bill.
So we have our code,
that could be in a git repository in Azure Repos,
that could go ahead and trigger a bill
whenever a commit is made onto the repository
that bill could happen in Azure Pipelines.
And now we should go ahead
and look at how do we deploy our bill?
The continuous bill process is part
of a process known as continuous integration.
And when you're looking at
deploying your application on a continual basis
you are looking towards a concept of continuous delivery.
So in the end, your application needs to be
posted on some sort of infrastructure because
you want users to be able to access your application.
For example, if you have a web application
that has been built with the help of Azure Pipelines,
you now need to go ahead and deploy the application.
When it comes onto the Azure platform
you could deploy your application on an
Azure Virtual Machine that's infrastructure has a service.
Or you could look at a platform as a service,
for example, Azure web apps.
Or maybe you wanna go ahead and deploy it on
a container base service, such as Azure Kubernetes.
Again, it depends upon the requirement of your application.
Now, apart from this, before we go ahead onto looking at
how do we manage releases for your application,
or basically deployment of your application.
Why Azure Pipelines?
Wanna give another important point
when it comes onto the delivery process.
Normally for your application,
you might first deploy the application
on a test environment, that is test infrastructure.
Here you might want to go ahead
and conduct any sort of basic testing
for the application just to make sure that it works.
Then from there, you might go ahead
and deploy your application on a staging environment.
Here you'd have a small subset of your
end users who would go ahead and test the application,
test the functionality of the application,
and once they sign off, when they say everything is okay,
then you might go ahead and publish the application
on production based infrastructure.
So before going ahead,
wanna have kind of an introduction first,
on the delivery process for your application.

--- Approvals and gates ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi, and welcome back.
Now, in the prior chapter,
we had seen how we could have
the deployment of an application onto multiple stages,
one was on a test environment stage
and the other was on a staging environment.
Now, obviously, in a real time scenario,
you will not have these deployments done
one after the other immediately.
First, the deployment would be done
onto a test environment,
then testing would be conducted
for, let's say, two to three days.
It depends upon the test scenarios.
And once testing is complete,
once an approval is sought from the test team,
then it could probably move over
onto the staging environment.
So there is some sort of delay between the deployment,
between both of these stages.
In our particular case now,
when the deployment was done,
immediately after the deployment onto the test environment,
it deployed the artifact onto the staging environment.
So we need to have some sort of pause in between
to signify that, please wait,
let testing be conducted, you know,
and then go ahead on to the next stage.
We can actually have different sort of measures in place.
We can use something known as approval and gates
to manage the transition between different stages.
We have something known as a pre-deployment approval
wherein a user needs to validate the change request
so that deployment can be done onto a particular stage.
For example,
here, the deployment onto the test environment itself.
You can have a pre-deployment approval
wherein someone needs to approve
the deployment of the artifact
onto this particular stage.
You can also have a post-deployment approval.
This could be attached onto the end of the deploy
on a test environment stage.
Here, a user needs to sign up.
So once testing is complete,
then a user can approve the movement of the artifact
from this stage onto the next.
So you can have approvals in place
to signify the transition of artifacts
on stage to stage.
Apart from this, you can also make use of gates.
See, these are all manual approaches.
But if you want to have sort of an automate approach,
you can make use of gates.
For example, let's say a user wants to ensure
that no work items are active for a particular build.
See what happens to that in Azure Boards?
Remember, you'll have tasks in place.
Those tasks will correlate onto a developer,
making a code change onto a GIT repository.
That change will be committed.
It will trigger a build,
and the build will trigger a release.
Now, let's say that the build
has been deployed onto the test environment
and you have bugs that have been found out,
and they've been registered in Azure Boards.
Now, before the build can go onto the next stage,
you wanna ensure that all of those bugs,
those work items in Azure Boards are closed.
Only then it should move onto the next stage.
This is an automated approach
instead of someone giving an approval,
as your pipelines can actually quickly go ahead
and see if those work items are opened or closed.
If they are closed,
then the build can move on to the next stage.
Like pre-deployment gates,
you also have post-deployment gates.
Again, this is returning onto issues.
This could be issues in your production-based environment.
So you might have,
let's say, alerts triggered in the Azure Monitor service,
and if alerts are in place,
then build should not be moved on to the next stage.
Now, obviously in the subsequent chapter,
we are gonna look at examples on approvals and gates,
in this chapter,
just wanna give kind of an introduction
on to this concept of having approvals and gates
for your pipelines.

--- Lab - Release pipelines - Approvals ---

(groovy music)
-: So in this chapter,
I want to go through a very simple example
when it comes onto approvals for a particular pipeline.
Go onto web app deployment and click on Edit.
For the first stage,
we have something known as pre-deployment conditions,
let me click on this.
In terms of the trigger,
it's after the release of the artifact
onto the artifact stage.
If I scroll down,
we have something known as pre-deployment approvals,
let me enable this.
We can search for users.
I'll search for TOC user 1.
This is the additional user I have
as part of my DevOps project.
We have something known as a timeout
that is also very important.

--- The next step - Containers ---

the basic understanding of Docker and how do you use Docker.
In my courses when it comes onto AZ 204,
and even if you're looking in general,
when it comes onto working with a .NET based application
and then publishing it onto a container-based system,
like Kubernetes or Azure Container Instances,
there is a list of steps that you need to perform.
So let's say we have a .NET-based application
as we've normally been using a ASP.NET application.
Every one you deployed has a container.
We first need to ensure that we containerize
our .NET-based application.
For this, we need to have Docker installed on a machine
so that we can containerize that application.
So we'll probably go through this entire list of steps.
The first step is, let's say,
to install Docker on a virtual machine.
Then we would publish a build of our application
onto that virtual machine that has Docker installed.
With the help of a Docker file,
we can create something known as a Custom Image.
This image is going to have our application in place.
We can then publish that image onto a private registry.
This could be the a Azure Container Registry Service.
And then we could use that image to deploy a container
either onto the Azure Container Instant Service,
or onto Azure Kubernetes.
We want to now have the ability
to deploy a container-based on an image
via the use of Azure Pipelines.
I wanna go step-by-step,
and first we'll have a revision also.
During this journey, using the manual process
so I will manually also first create, just as a revision,
an image based on a .NET based application that we have.
This will then help us understand
how do we do the same via use of Azure Pipelines.
So let's first start with the manual process
of how do we containerize a .NET based application.

--- Revision - Building a Docker image ---

--- About Container jobs ---

(music)
-: Hi and welcome back.
Now, in this chapter, I want to go through container jobs.
Now up till point,
when we were looking at, you know,
building our application.
So let's take the case of us building our applications
on a Microsoft hosted agent, or even on self hosted agent.
Now our build runs inside a compute instance
and for us we only be running one build at a time.
Now if you're taking the case
of let's say a self-hosted agent,
you might be reusing that same infrastructure
for having multiple bills
of maybe multiple applications in place.
You might be having the required tools
that need to be installed.
So I said you might be having different applications
that would be building on the same machine.
If this is the case, there could be a scenario where in
because you need to install so many tools,
so many maybe you know, packages
on that same machine to help the building
of the different applications, there might be a conflict.
So you might want to isolate the build process
of each application.
In this case,
what you can do is you can make use of container jobs.
So here you are using the concept
of running everything within a container.
Now, don't confuse this with what we have done earlier on.
Earlier on, we were running an application
as a container within the image.
Your DevOps, Azure DevOps is providing you
the functionality of running your build within a container.
And I said, Why would they want to do this?
So I said, let's say you have a self hosted agent in place.
You have builds of multiple applications
running on the same machine.
So maybe you want to save on cost,
you don't have multiple self hosted agent.
Now each application, when it comes onto the build,
they might be requiring different tools
or packages to be installed.
And I told you, in case
if there is a conflict that's happening
between these packages because you might be having
different package versions
that need to be installed for each application.
You want to isolate the entire build process
on the same machine.
Well, welcome to containers again,
because with the use of containers
you can have that isolated environment.
So now your build can actually run
within containers on the machine itself.
And I said, don't confuse this
with having your .net application running as containers.
No.
Here, it is your entire job
that's running within a container
that's managed by Azure DevOps.
You can just have a normal application running
on that container job.
So in the next chapter,
let's look at an example on how we can accomplish this.

--- Quick Note on Technical Debt ---

(upbeat music)
-: Now, in this chapter, I just wanna give a quick note
when it comes on to technical debt.
Now, initially when you develop your application
you know you might be taking all of the right measures
in place to ensure that you have quality
in place for development of the application.
But then you know, after it is promoted to production,
when you start adding features, at that point in time,
because of time constraints, because of budget constraints,
developers may not have enough time
to incorporate quality when it comes
onto coding these additional features.
Now, because of this, you could be having code
that may not be structured in a particular way
might not be following the right standards.
And then over time, when developers want to start
making more and more changes onto the application
it could start becoming difficult to make these changes.
And this could be because of the quality of code
that was put in place
as part of the previous application changes,
it's not up to the mark.
Because obviously over time
if you don't look after the quality of your code
the maintainability of the code becomes difficult.
This can actually be tracked by the solar cloud tool.
When it comes on to maintainability,
it also keeps a track of the technical debt.
It has something known as code smells,
wherein it looks at code that is confusing
and difficult to maintain,
it can give you these sort of attributes.
So, something that it should also consider,
wherein using the code quality tool
to ensure that the technical debt does not increase
by your overall code base.
Just wanna kind of give this important note
when it comes onto code quality.

--- Modularize templates ---

(upbeat music)
-: Now before we can look at
how to use ARM Templates as part of Azure Pipelines,
I want to go through nested and linked ARM templates
because it's part of the exam objectives.
Now in our case,
when it came on to building the ARM Template,
it's quite simple.
We don't have that many resources in place.
But sometimes if your application architecture,
your infrastructure, is quite complex in nature,
wherein you have a lot of resources that need to be deployed
it could sometimes become, you know,
difficult from a maintainability perspective
to put all of the resources within one template itself.
You could consider actually breaking your template
into multiple pieces.
And you can do this with the help of either
creating a nested or a linked template.
In a nested template, you define the resources,
the nested resources within the template itself.
So I'll show you an example on this.
Or another way is to define your resources
within another template itself,
and then link it in the parent template.
Now, by having linked templates,
you now have the concept of modules in place
wherein, you know, you could have some core resources
that need to be deployed
in multiple application environments.
So you could define that in one template,
and then reference that template
in multiple parent templates itself.
Now before we go on to nested and linked templates,
just a quick note when it comes onto deployment modes.
When you deploy a template,
there are some deployment modes in place.
You have the incremental mode.
Here, the resources within the template are deployed.
It does not interfere with other resources
that are defined in the resource group
which are not defined in the template.
See, in our case, we had one resource group Template GRP.
We were then using a template, let's say,
to deploy an Azure web app and an Azure app service plan
within that resource group.
By default, this is not going to interfere
with other resources within the resource group
that are not defined in the template.
So let's say we already had resources
within that resource group.
The incremental mode will ensure that it does not tamper
or do anything with other resources
within that resource group.
Whereas, if you do the complete mode,
here, only the resources defined within the template
will be defined in the resource group.
If there are other resources within the resource group
that are not managed by the ARM Template,
they will be deleted.
When it comes on to nested and linked templates,
the only mode that is available is the incremental mode.
Now, let's go on to seeing examples
when it comes onto nested and linked templates...

--- About Terraform ---

-: Hi, and welcome back.
Now, in these subsequent chapters,
we're gonna see how we can use Terraform
to build our infrastructure as part of our pipeline.
Early on, we had seen ARM templates,
and we saw Azure CLI commands.
Now let's look at Terraform.
In the next chapter, I'll show you how to set up Terraform.
Then we'll see how to build simple infrastructure
like our Azure apps and Azure SQL database,
via the use of Terraform configuration.
And we'll see our report as part of our pipeline.
Again, the concept is similar.
Terraform is used to deploy your infrastructure.
So if you have, let's say,
an application server or a database server,
you wanna deploy and configure your infrastructure,
you can do it as Infrastructure as code.
Again, you have a code file is actually a configuration file
within Terraform which can be used to deploy your resources.
Again, you can change the code whenever required.
You can share the code, you can create different versions
of your code.
And what makes Terraform very popular is,
it not only works with Azure,
you can use it to deploy resources
on Amazon web services on Google Cloud, et cetera.
There's a lot of functionality available within Terraform.
It works with a variety of cloud providers.
The code is in a human readable format.
It has an avid community.
It's an open source project.
So a lot of benefits when it comes
onto the usage of Terraform.
Now, just a quick note, when it comes onto the workflow,
the basic workflow for Terraform, the first step
is to go ahead and write your Terraform configuration file.
This basically defines the resources
that need to be deployed.
So let's say you deploy an application server
and a database server as part of your infrastructure.
You first define the definition of those resources
within your Terraform configuration file.
Let's say in your target environment
you don't have these resources in place.
You define it first in your Terraform configuration file.
Then you create something known as a Terraform plan.
The Terraform plan will actually look
at your configuration file and decide
on the resources that need to be deployed or changed.
It does this via the use of a state file.
And then finally, you do a Terraform apply.
When you do a Terraform apply,
it will apply all of the changes
has by your Terraform configuration file.
This is when deploy, lets your application server
and your database server onto your environment.
We're gonna be using the Terraform CLI.
You just simply download a Terraform binary file
on your local machine, on your laptop basically.
You develop your configuration file
and then you can go ahead
and plan and apply the configuration file accordingly.
So if you look at Terraform, there's actually
an open source tool that's developed by HashiCorp.
It helps you automate and manage your infrastructure.
It uses a declarative language when it comes
onto defining your code,
and you can use it to build infrastructure
on multiple cloud environments.
So let's look at how we can use Terraform
to build our infrastructure.

--- Desired State Configuration ---

(upbeat music playing)
-: Hi, and welcome back.
Now, in the subsequent chapter,
we are gonna look at
an example on the implementation of something known as
the Desired State Configuration.
What is the purpose of Desired State Configuration?
Now, let's say you are hosting a web application
on an Azure virtual machine or a set of virtual machines.
For that web application to be hosted, you might need to
have internet information services always installed
on the machine for hosting the web application.
You have to ensure that IS,
information services, is always in place.
And
if by any chance,
someone has gone ahead and uninstall
the role itself,
automatically,
again, internet information services should be in place.
So, you want to ensure that the configuration of the machine
does not drift, or it could impact the application.
For this, you can make use of
PowerShell Desired State Configuration.
Here, you are trying to ensure the state configuration
of a node such as an Azure virtual machine
stays as it should be.
When it comes onto the implementation of PowerShell DSC,
we are gonna look at service or a resource
known as Azure automation.
Azure automation actually has an inbuilt server
that can ensure that all machines receive
the desired configuration.
And you can manage all of the configuration and the nodes
from Azure portal itself.
In this chapter, want to give a quick introduction
onto Design State Configuration.
In the subsequent chapter,
we are gonna look now at an example on
how we can implement this.

--- Virtual Machine Scale Sets ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi and welcome back.
Now, I want to go through
how we can deploy virtual machine scale sets.
Why the use of an ARM template?
Again, coming from any exam background
you'll be having an idea
when it comes onto the virtual machine scale set.
It's used for scaling your virtual machine infrastructure
whenever it is required.
When the demand is high,
it will increase the number of virtual machines,
where the demand is low,
it'll decrease the number of virtual machines.
Because let's take an example
of an application that is being hosted on Azure VM.
Now, when the load on the VM increases
because the number of users probably using the application
is increasing.
You might want to add one machine onto this entire portfolio
so that the application load can be distributed
across the Azure VMs.
But this is a manual intervention.
You can make use of the virtual machine scale set,
which can automatically increase the number of machines
in the scale set, that is hosting your application.
You can create conditions based on metrics to decide
when to increase the number of machines in your scale set.
You can also put your scale set machines
sitting behind an Azure load balancer,
so that the Azure load balancer can do the job
of distributing the request from users
across your Azure virtual machines
that are running as part of the virtual machine scale set.
So I said we are gonna be looking
at an ARM template deployment,
when it comes onto the virtual machine scale set.
