--- The fear of changes ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi and welcome back.
Now, in the next set of chapters
we're gonna go through examples
when it comes onto deployment strategies
for updates to an application.
See, let's say that you have an application in place
that is being hosted on an Azure web app
and it's connecting onto an Azure SQL database.
Now, the user basically asks for some changes,
to the application, maybe new features need to be added,
and you need to make some code changes
and then promote those code changes
onto the Azure DevOp, basically onto your web applications.
Now, there's always a fear factor in place
when changes need to be made onto an existing application,
because what happens if the change fails?
If the code that has been published,
the new code that has been published, stops the application,
than the application is not accessible
onto customers and they're not happy with what has happened.
And then this becomes a risk onto the business
because customers are not happy
with the problems that are occurring
when changes are being made onto an application.
That is why companies strive a lot
to make changes as seamless, as possible.
So, there's always that fear factor in place
when it comes on to making changes to an application.
That's why companies employ
different sort of deployment strategies
when it comes on to making these application changes.
And, this is something that we're gonna look
in the subsequent chapters.

--- Blue-Green Deployments ---

(Introduction Music)
-: Now in this chapter,
I want to go through a deployment strategy
that's known as the blue-green deployment.
In this strategy, again
let's say you have a web application in place
it is being hosted on an Azure web app.
Now all of your users are directed
onto this particular environment.
This is your current production environment.
Sometimes this can also be termed
as the blue environment when all of your users
are currently targeting the application
that is running on this environment.
So let's say the developers had made changes
to the application and the basic tests have been conducted.
In order to make sure that the application
the new application version works as desired,
you can build up a new environment
known as the green environment
that'll have the newer version of your application running.
This kind of a duplicate setup
but you have the newer application version running here.
All of your users are still using the older version
of the application in the current production environment.
Then on the green environment, you'll do some sanity checks.
You know you'll do some basic checks.
Once the testing is complete, the basic checks are complete,
you will then switch your users to go ahead
and access the green environment
the newer application version environment.
This now becomes your current production environment.
There are mechanisms in place in which you can switch
over your users from one environment onto the other.
It depends on what is a service you are using
when it comes on to directing traffic to your application
On Azure, there are various ways
you can actually go about doing this.
When you're using a blue-green deployment,
one of the biggest advantage is the quick switch over
that you can make for users
from one environment onto the other.
You are not looking at a long downtime when it comes
on to promoting a newer application version.
See, let's say you don't have this concept
of the blue-green deployment in place.
You just have one environment and now you need
to publish a newer version of the application.
Sometimes, in order to promote this newer version
what you might need to do first is you might need
to stop the web application
so that it doesn't take current request from your users.
So this is a downtime for the application.
Then you might take backups of, let's say the data
or anything else just to make sure that you have a backup
in place and then you'll promote
the newer version of your application.
You will then do sanity checks just to make sure
that the application is working as expected
and then you can make your users
access the newer version of this application.
So this could be a downtime anywhere from
let's say, 15 minutes to half an hour.
Again, depending upon the application itself.
In this particular situation
when you're using a blue-green deployment
it becomes much more faster to switch the users
from one environment onto the other
so the downtime is decreased.
Another advantage is, let's say
in the extreme situation, the green environment
the new environment is not working, as expected.
You still have the older environment in place.
You can do a switch back very easily
onto the older version of the application just to ensure
that the users can still access the application
without any issues.
One of the downsides
of the Blue Green environment is obviously the cost
because at any point in time
you'll be having two environments
in place when in fact only one of them is gonna be accessed.
And if you have a complex architecture
when it comes on to your application
then you'll have more infrastructure components
that will be running at a time, and that means more cost.
It all depends on how critical your application is.
If you have a revenue generating application that's critical
in order to reduce the amount of downtime
then you can go ahead
and think about using the deployment strategy
of blue-green deployments.
Now, in the next chapter, I'll show you an aspect
or a feature that's available
when it comes onto Azure web apps
when it comes onto magic blue-green deployments.

--- Azure Web App - Deployment Slots ---

(upbeat music)
-: Now, we wanna look at an example

we can make use of deployment slots.

you'll all be familiar with the use of deployment slots.

In the next chapter,
we look at a quick lab
on working with deployment slots.
Again, this will be a revision.
And then, in the subsequent chapter
we'll see how to use deployment slots
when it comes onto Azure pipelines.
With the feature of deployment slots
in your App Service plans,
which are attached onto your web apps,
you can have staging environments in place
when it comes onto your web applications.
So, when you have an Azure Web App in place,
by default it'll have a production slot.
And let's say, you've deployed version one
of your application.
When you want to deploy the next version
of the application itself,
you can create something known as a slot
within the Azure Web App itself.
This is like creating an entire, new Azure Web App area,
in which you can publish the new version
of your application.
You can then validate all of the application changes
in that staging deployment slot,
and then you can perform a quick swap
between the staging and the production slot.
So at one point in time,
your users will be accessing
let's say, a version one of the application.
When you perform a swap of these slots,
users will then be accessing version two
of your application.
This eliminates the downtime
for the application when new changes are being deployed.
And obviously, you can do a swap back.
I said, in case version two of your application
is not working as expected,
you can always roll back the changes.
Again, perform a swap.
This feature just has a note,
when it comes onto your deployment slots,
is only available in the Standard App Service Plan
or higher.
So, you can't use the Free App Service Plan.
You can't use a Shared, you can't use a Basic.
You need to have the Standard App Service Plan or higher.

let's have, again, a quick clap, a quick review,
when it comes onto deployment slots.

--- Canary deployments ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi and welcome back.
In this chapter, I just briefly wanna go through
another type of deployment that you have.
This is known as a canary deployment.
Again, let's consider that you have an application.
This is running in your production environment,
and all of your users are accessing the application itself.
Now, when a new feature needs to be made available
for the application to your users,
when it comes onto a canary deployment,
this new feature is made available
only onto a subset of users.
So maybe only 10% of your users
will be able to see the new feature of your application.
Now, there are multiple ways
in which you could go about doing this.
You could have another environment in place,
again a duplicate environment of your application
which has the newer feature,
and then direct a percentage of these users
onto the newer environment.
The feature can be tested out,
and then maybe gradually you can move all of your users
onto the newer environment,
or you could have featured toggles within your application.
You can have, this is something that you need to embed
within your application,
where you can just have a simple on and off switch
when it comes on to enabling features for certain users.
But yeah, the entire idea is kind of a gradual way
of implementing the newer feature
for your application to your users.
Initially, you'll have few users who'll have access
onto that new feature,
and if it is working as expected,
it can then be rolled out onto all of your users
who are using your application.
Now in these subsequent chapters,
we are gonna be looking at another routing tool
that's available in Azure,
that is the Azure Traffic Manager,
and how it can be used
when it comes on to building up deployment strategy.

--- Azure Traffic Manager ---

(upbeat music)
-: Now in the subsequent chapters, we are gonna see
how we can use Azure Traffic Manager.
When it comes onto routing traffic,
Azure Traffic Manager is a DNS-based load balancer.
Here you can distribute traffic
to public-facing applications
across different Azure regions.
And in Azure Traffic Manager,
you have something known as routing methods
that allow you to route traffic.
Now, if you're coming from an Azure background,
you might be having knowledge
on the working of Azure Traffic Manager.
In the next, chapter we'll have kind of a revision,
where you will have a lab on the implementation
of Azure Traffic Manager, and subsequently
we'll see how to use this particular service
when it comes onto a Azure Pipeline.
In Azure Traffic Manager, you have,
I said different sort of routing methods,
for example, if you choose the priority routing method
for an Azure Traffic Manager profile.
You can have multiple apps
sitting behind Azure Traffic Manager.
This could be hosting your web application.
Your users will be directed first
onto the Traffic Manager profile,
and then based on the routing method,
the Traffic Manager profile will direct users
onto the backend end-points.
If you choose the priority routing method,
you can choose one application as the primary target
when it comes onto the request.
So let's say that you have a priority of one.
When it comes onto an Azure web app
running in the North Europe location,
all of your users will be directed
onto that Azure web app.
You can then have another Azure web app
running in a different location.
You can give this as a priority of two.
If for example, something goes wrong
with the application running in the primary region,
then Azure Traffic Manager can start directing request
onto the application, the Azure web app,
that has the second priority
running in a different location.
So this is like an active-passive setup,
in case if your first set-up, your first Azure web app
is not running as expected,
let's say the North Europe location has gone down,
the services have gone down,
the Traffic Manager profile runs at the global level,
so it is not impacted by North Europe region going down.
Then the Traffic Manager profile can direct requests
onto the Azure web app running in a different location.
Another example of another routing method
is the weighted routing method.
This can be useful when it comes
onto your canary deployments.
Here you will have your applications,
again running on multiple Azure web apps,
in different locations.
In the weighted routing method, you can give a weightage
onto the end-points that are running behind the profile.
For example, you can give a weightage
of let's say 1,000 onto the application
running in the North Europe location,
and a weightage of just one to the web app
running in the UK South location.
That means almost all of your users will be directed
onto the Azure web app that has a higher weightage.
Let's say in the canary deployment.
you have deployed a new feature onto this Azure web app,
and you want a subset of your users
to be directed onto this web application,
you can increase the weightage accordingly.
You can probably put this as 100,
you can leave this as 1,000.
What the Traffic Manager profile will do is,
it will calculate the percentage
based on the weightage and direct the traffic accordingly.
And if you want now all of your users
to be directed onto the new application
which has the new features, you can put this as a 1,000,
and you can put this as one,
you can change the weightage accordingly.
So, there are multiple routing methods in place,
when it comes onto the Azure Traffic Manager service,
based on your routing needs,
you can choose the routing method accordingly.

--- Using a rolling deployment ---

(upbeat lively music)
-: Hi and welcome back.
Now in this chapter
I just want to give a quick note
when it comes onto rolling deployments.
Actually this is a strategy
that we've already seen early on.
Let's say that we have an application
that is hosted on a set of Azure virtual machines.
And let's say we want to now update the version
of the application running on these Azure virtual machines.
We had seen the concept of deployment groups
early on in our pipelines
and at that point in time
the deployment group could apply the update
onto the machines one at a time
or you know batches at a time
not all at once.
So you are doing it in phases.
This could be done with deployment groups.
You can also have this rolling updates when it comes
onto Azure virtual machines.
Another version of the rolling updates is
let's say again you have machines
that are hosting an application
when you want to roll out a new version of your application.
So this is when it comes onto the virtual machine scale set.
New machines with the new application version
would be spun up and it would gradually replace the machines
which have the older version of your application.
When you are doing it in such a manner
you have to remember that at any point in time
during the upgrade process
you would have machines running
both the older and the newer application versions.
So in this chapter is wanna kind of give this
quick note when it comes onto rolling deployment.

--- Using a Load Balancer ---

(upbeat funky music)
Presenter: Hi and welcome back.
Now I want to go through how you can
use the Azure load balancer when it
comes on to managing your Blue-Green Deployments.
You probably would've seen articles
in which you can use a load balancing service to
direct traffic accordingly when it comes
onto your blue and green environments.
So I wanna give you an example
on how this can be achieved.
Again, for those who might be familiar
with the Azure Load Balancer,
just a quick revision when it comes
onto the purpose of the load balancing service.
So you could have your applications being hosted
on a set of Azure virtual machines.
If you want to load balance traffic from your users
across the virtual machines hosting the application
you can make use of an Azure Load Balancer.
You will add the machines to your backend pool.
These are the machines hosting your application.
We then configure aspects such
as a help probe on the load balancer.
The help probe will understand
if the backend virtual machines are healthy or not
and you can define load balancing rules
on how you would load balance traffic,
the incoming traffic from the users
across the Azure virtual machines.
You configure the Azure virtual machine
as part of a backend pool that sits
behind the Azure load balancer.
When it comes onto a Blue-Green Deployment
let's say you have an application just for the sake
of simplicity running on one Azure virtual machine.
This is your blue environment.
All views are directed
onto this current production environment.
Please note, you can have multiple machines in place.
You can configure your load balancer to ensure
that initially all traffic from the users
is directed onto this environment.
Then you can deploy your new application version
on another Azure virtual machine.
This is gonna be part of your green environment
and then when you're ready to make the switch
you can make your load balancer point onto the VM
or the VMs running as part of your green environment.
So in the next chapter, I'm gonna show you an example
on how you can actually perform this particular switch.

--- Package Management ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi, and welcome back.
Now, we are going to go into
understanding about
the tool which is available as part of Azure DevOps
and that's Azure Artifacts.
Azure Artifacts allows developers to go ahead
and publish their code as packages
so that they can be used by, basically, other developers
within their organization.
See, when you're talking about packages,
here it is giving an example of
an application that we have,
or a project that we have, in Visual Studio 2022.
Now, let's say I want to use functionality
when it comes onto working with JSON based data.
Now, instead of
the developer
creating this code by themselves,
they should look online
if code already exists
that can help them work with JSON.
So, in fact, there is a package available
if I go onto Tools, NuGet Package Manager,
Manage NuGet Packages for the Solution.
If I go onto Browse and search for JSON,
there is a package known as Newtonsoft.Json.
When you install this package as part of your project,
it then allows you to
use the code functionality
that is available in this package.
So, if you want to serialize JSON data,
de-serialize JSON data,
instead of the developer writing the code from scratch,
they can make use of an existing package
that already has that functionality in place.
They can then focus on their main business logic code
instead of worrying about this utility
that's already available as a package.
When it comes onto .net,
so you have NuGet.org which has all of these packages.
Visual Studio is going onto
NuGet to fetch all of the packages.
Similarly, for other programming languages,
you have other package solutions in place.
Now, when it comes onto NuGet,
these packages are available on the internet.
Any developer from Visual Studio,
or if they just have NuGet in place,
they can go ahead and fetch the package.
But let's say that an organization
wants more control
when it comes onto their packages,
or maybe they want to have a private repository
for the packages their developers create.
So, maybe you might be having a central team
that is creating code packages that can be used
by other applications that are developed
by other development teams within the organization itself.
Instead of making those code packages available
on an internet based site,
they only want to be in a repository
within the organization itself.
For this purpose, one thing that you can do
is to make use of Azure Artifacts.
So, it's like having your own
package management solution,
but it's instead onto Azure DevOps
and you can use it accordingly.
So, I said when it comes onto Azure Artifacts,
developers can go ahead and
publish their packages onto Azure Artifacts.
So, whether you need NuGet packages,
which is for .net,
or npm packages for JavaScript,
or Maven packages for Java, or Python based packages,
you can make use of Azure Artifacts.
Your developers can publish their packages.
Developers can also consume their packages,
and developers can additionally also
consume their packages from public feeds as well.
So, in this chapter, I just want to give
kind of an introduction when it comes
onto the Azure Artifact service.

--- Note - Flaky tests ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi, and welcome back.
Now, in this chapter,
I just want to give a quick note
when it comes on to flaky tests.
See, flaky test means that those tests,
which often pass,
but at some points in time,
they could fail,
even though there was no major change done
in the source code
or the execution environment
that actually would cause
that particular pipeline to fail.
So sometimes the pipeline fails,
even though at that point in time it would idly pass.
This is also known as a flaky test.
See, sometimes maybe when you're doing a build,
maybe the pipeline depends upon an external service,
a connection onto an external service.
And let's say, that external service is down.
So, even though there was not much change done
onto your source code,
but at that point in time,
because the external service was not available,
that's why the pipeline failed.
So, this doesn't give you an accurate picture
when it comes onto your pipeline runs.
There is an option available in Azure DevOps
that allows you to automatically detect
such sort of failures as flaky tests.
If you go onto your project settings,
and under test management
you have the flaky test detection,
which is marked as on.
You are using system detection.
Yeah, Azure pipelines can detect for flaky tests.
If you scroll down, it is enabled for all pipelines.
If you scroll further down,
you can also include the flaky test
in your test pass percentage.
So, I just wanna kind of give this quick note
when it comes onto this feature,
when it comes onto test management
for your Azure DevOps projects.

--- Review of service connections ---

(upbeat rhythmic music)
-: (instructor) In this chapter, just wanna give a quick note
when it comes on to service connections.
This is something we've already seen during this course.
When we want to connect onto an external service
we make use of a service connection.
If you go onto the project settings within your project
you have these service connections.
Here we have a number of service connections in place.
So when as Azure DevOps
wants to connect on to let's say your Azure subscription
or onto GitHub
or onto an Azure Kubernetes cluster
you can create a service connection
and then use it within your pipeline.
Instead of embedding the credentials
for connecting to an external service
within your pipeline itself
you can create a secure connection via service connections,
wherein the credentials for connecting
onto that external service is
within the service connection itself,
and all you have to do
is to reference that service connection
within your pipeline.
So again, we have already seen this is very simple.
Within your project,
if you want to connect onto an external service
you make use of service connections.

onto an external service.

So the other way around
and incoming connection onto Azure DevOps.

you can generate a personal access token

it could use a personal access token.
Or when it wanna become part of a deployment group.
When you create a new personal access token
you decide what is the access you are giving
to that personal access token within Azure DevOps.
So when something needs to connect onto Azure DevOps
onto a particular feature
one way if it is available
is to make use of personal access tokens.

--- Azure Monitor - Setting up alerts ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi, and welcome back.
In this chapter, want to revise the use of alerts
when it comes onto the Azure Monitoring Service.
For example, if you want your IT administrative team
to be notified if any sort of metric goes
beyond a certain threshold.
For example let's say your web application is facing errors.
The users are receiving HTTP responses that correlates
onto errors for accessing the web application.
You can have a metric in place accordingly
and then probably send an email
or notify an IT administrative team.
Or if the CPU percentage of a virtual machine goes
beyond a particular threshold, you can do that as well.
When you create an alert rule,
the first thing is you define the scope.
For which resource do you want to base the alert rule on?
If I filter on all of my resources, so for example
if I take a virtual machine that I have and I click on Done
first step is to define the resource.
The next step, very important, is to define the condition.
You can define the condition based on metrics.
Metrics, that is the CPU being consumed.
You can base it on activity log,
if any sort of operation is performed on the machine.
You can base it on log.
This is a custom log search.
If I click on Custom Log Search,
it will take me onto a Log Analytics workspace.
Now, currently, there's no data found because I don't have
any Log Analytics workspace that actually collecting data
about this Azure virtual machine.
Going back onto creating an alert rule,
(mouse clicking)
so we were on the condition.
And then we have the resource, health of the VM itself.
(mouse clicking)
Here now, let's say I go onto the metrics.
And let's take the metric of the percentage CPU.
If I scroll down, we have two thresholds in place.
One is static, and the other is dynamic.
Next, we have the operator where we can say
if the average CPU percentage is greater than 70%
then ensure to trigger an alert.
Next, we have what is a frequency of evaluation.
The frequency of evaluation is one minute.
At this point in time, it's defined as one minute.
This means that the alert rule is going to check
every one minute whether the average CPU percentage is
greater than 70%.
If I put the frequency of evaluation as every five minutes,
this means the alert rule will check now
once every five minutes
if the percentage CPU is going beyond 70%.
This is the frequency of evaluation.
Next is the aggregation granularity.
Here when you're talking about five minutes,
you are telling the Azure Monitoring Service
when we are evaluating this alert rule
take the data, the average data, over the last five minutes
to come to a conclusion
whether the CPU percentage is going beyond 70%.
If I choose this has 15 minutes,

--- What is Application Insights ---

(upbeat intro music)
-: Hi and welcome back.
Now let's have a review of Application Insights
that if you're coming from a background
of AZ-104 or AZ-204,
you'll be very familiar with this particular service.
So this will be a quick review.
We'll also see, again, a quick example
on how you can set up Application Insights
for those who are not familiar with the service itself.
So Application Insights provides the feature
when it comes on to application performance management
and monitoring of your live web applications.
Here you can see aspects
that it's detecting performance issues
or any other issues when it comes onto your application.
There is support for .Net, for Note.gs, Java, and Python.
This can work for applications
that are hosted in Azure
on your on premises environment or other cloud platforms
as well.
It has integration with Visual Studio,
has an integrate development environment
and here you can see several aspects,
such as how your users interact with your application.
So, how does it normally work?
Well, you install a small instrumentation package
that is an SDK for your application
or you can use the Application Insights agent.
You can then instrument your web application,
your background components,
and even your JavaScript in your web pages.
The telemetric data send by Application Insights
has very little impact when comes
onto the overall performance of your application.
So in the next chapter,
let's look at an example where it comes
onto the usage of Application Insights.

For example, if I look at the data in the app request table
and I click on run.
You will see information about all of the get requests
that we had seen early on
when it came onto the performance section
for Application Insights.

when it comes onto Application Insights by default
is being recorded in a Log Analytics workspace.

but Application Insights gives you a way

the data for Application Insights

--- Note - Azure Container Instances probes ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi, and welcome back.
Now, in this chapter
I just wanna give a quick note
when it comes onto the probes
that you can configure for your containers
when they are being deployed
on to Azure Container Instances.
You can actually use YAML files
when it comes onto the deployment
of your container instances.
This is done by the use of container groups.
Now, there's something known as a Liveness Probe
wherein by you scroll on onto the YAML deployment.
When you're doing the deployment itself,
you can specify that you want to have this probe in place
and what is appeared in seconds.
See, what this does is,
let's say your container is not working for some reason.
It can go ahead and actually restart
the container automatically.
This is very important if you have functionality
within the container that needs to be up and running,
and let's say it's crashed because of some error
you can go ahead and ensure
that the container instance
can restart the container automatically.
Another probe that is available is the Readiness Probe.
If I scroll down, so let's say that your container,
your application container, depends upon a database
being up and running and having data in place.
You can ensure that you have the Readiness Probe in place.
In the Readiness Probe,
you'll actually have commands in place to check,
once the data is in place,
then go ahead and start this particular container.
Because let's say you have an application running
in the container and it tries to connect to a database
which is not in the ready state,
the application will get an error.
So this is from the perspective of the exam.
Just wanted you all to know
on these two types of probes
that you can have for your container instances.

--- Note on IT Service Management Connector ---

(upbeat music)
-: Hi and welcome back.
Now in this chapter I just want give a quick note
when it comes onto the IT Service Management Connector.
When it comes on to Azure,
you have the alerts that can be defined for various metrics
that are being recorded in the Azure monitoring service.
But your company might be using an external tool
when it comes onto, let's say, incident management.
So if there is any sort of incident that actually occurs
in your production-based environment.
So let's say that an alert gets generated
while the use of the CPU going beyond a particular threshold
for your Azure virtual machine.
Now, you also wanna ensure as a company
that an incident, you know, a problem gets recorded
in the service management solution that they are using.
So they could be using ServiceNow
as a service management solution.
At that point in time
they might want to have an integration
between Azure Monitor and the service management solution.
They can make use of IT Service Management Connector
so they can connect their service management solution
onto Azure Monitor.
So whenever an alert gets triggered
within the Azure monitoring service,
it could create an incident
within their service management solution.
In this chapter, I wanna give this quick note
when it comes onto the IT Service Management Connector.
